### Scope of the ring defect; first-request and 16K leads after the fix (2026-09-25)

Scope. The defect is in upstream vLLM source, not in the Spark image or
hardware: `vllm/models/deepseek_v4_1/sparse_mla.py` `_tokens` gates every
cache on the generic slot mapping, which the runner leaves as PAD for any
`CircularBufferSpec`. At 1794dcf1 the other ring users handle this: the
sibling `vllm/models/deepseek_v41/compressor.py` builds ring slots itself, and
`qwen4_exp/common/qsa_cache.py` compiles its circular branch without the
common-slot gate (only its compressed branch keeps it). So among ring users at
this pin it is specific to the DS4.1 `deepseek_v4_1` B12X metadata path, on any
GPU that runs it.

Even lengths are affected too, later in a generation. The ring is read at any
step whose first position is odd. An even prompt's first verify starts even,
but after acceptance `a` the next real step starts at `L + a`, which is odd
whenever `a` is odd. Our 3-token probes end inside the first verify, so even
prompts never reached such a step; any longer generation would, including
the original 524K dual-needle failure, whose answer spans several decode
steps. The unfixed ring is therefore a plausible contributor to that original
failure, to be confirmed by replaying it on the fixed image.

First request after boot (128-0, and 385-0 on both later boots). Receipts and
one-second telemetry on three boots show the same shape:
- 14:12 boot, 128-0 (even; no ring read at its verify): 31.8 s, positions 1-2
  differ.
- 15:17 boot, 385-0: prefill 11.7 s, decode 18.7 s, prefill exact, positions
  1-2 differ. Decode window 15:18:07-15:18:26: NVMe reads on dusty first
  (48-72 MB/s), then on toby, rusty and kirby; dusty then sits flat at
  19.8 W and toby at 17.7 W for about 10 s (15:18:16-15:18:25) while kirby,
  still reading, stays near 13 W; it ends when the response returns.
- 15:57 boot, 385-0: prefill 23.3 s, decode 15.6 s, and the prefill itself
  differs (position 0). The prefill window has a flat, all-rank plateau of
  about 5 s (15:57:24-15:57:28, 18-20 W) that the 15:17 boot's exact prefill
  lacks; the decode window again shows staggered per-rank NVMe bursts and a
  10 s flat plateau (dusty 19.9 W, 15:57:52-15:58:01).
The plateaus come in 5 s and 10 s units, one or two 5000 ms Engram waits,
and a prefill plateau appears only on the boot whose prefill diverged. That
is the silent `_wait_engram_rows` timeout path (5 s, `failed` never read, the
step proceeds on unready `staged_rows`), triggered by cold first-use lookups.
It explains the even-length 128-0 case that the ring cannot. Inferred from
timing and telemetry, not yet observed directly.

The 16K outlier (full sweep, 16384-1, 8.06 s, all positions differ): the four
ranks show no NVMe reads and symmetric power in its window
(14:16:33-14:16:41), and a 10.4 s repeat elsewhere matched. There is no disk
signature, so it is not the cold-lookup case; a host-side stall of the lookup
thread past the wait remains possible but unsupported. Keep it open.

Discriminators, cheapest first:
1. On the fixed image, per boot, make the first request an even-length
   prompt (128) and repeat it; record the telemetry. Prediction: divergence
   of the first request together with the NVMe burst and a 5 s or 10 s
   plateau, and none afterwards.
2. Same boot pattern, but first send one unrelated request that touches
   different n-grams (cold lookups absorbed), then the 128 series. Prediction:
   128-0 then matches its repeats; that separates first-use lookup cost from
   "first request of the process".
3. Decisive without code: a boot with `VLLM_DS41_ENGRAM_OVERLAP=0`
   (synchronous lookups, no wait kernel); the first request must match its
   repeats even if slow. Needs a launch-contract amendment.
4. Direct: record the model's `_engram_epochs` (ready, expected, failed) after
   startup and after every request, resetting `failed` between. The stale
   probe does not capture it today (its extra state covers compressor and CED
   buffers only); adding `_engram_epochs` would need a helper change.
5. For 16K: long serial 16K series on the fixed image with telemetry and
   per-request prefill time; if outliers persist without disk activity, time
   the lookup job on the host (thread start and end) against the forward.

### Why the Engram wait reaches 5 s: dependency analysis and repair (2026-09-25)

Pinned sources read: vLLM 1794dcf1 `nvidia/model.py` (`prepare_disk_engram`,
`_start_engram_job`), `common/engram.py` (`_wait_engram_rows`, staging);
b12x a7d7d29b `sequence/engram/_impl.py` (`run_lookups`, `run_lookup`),
`_kernels.py` (`_lookup`, `lookup_op`), `_disk.py`,
`_shared/disk_table.py` (`DiskRowCache.transaction`, `_stage_ids`,
`_read_staged`).

What sits between the forward launch and the epoch the wait kernel spins on:
1. Main thread, in `prepare_disk_engram`: hash kernel on the main stream,
   `epochs[1].fill_(epoch)`, `ready.record()`, `pool.submit(lookup)`; then the
   forward (FULL graph for decode, eager for prefill) is launched and the
   Engram layers spin in `_wait_engram_rows` (5000 ms limit).
2. Side thread `lookup()` (Python): `stream.wait_event(ready)`; per table a
   `transaction()` (side stream waits the previous `_cache_done`);
   `_stage_ids` (D2H copy of hash ids on the side stream, record
   `_ids_ready`); `_read_staged` blocks the thread on
   `_ids_ready.synchronize()`, then `ple_reader_run` (native, GIL released);
   then, back in Python, `lookup_op` (torch custom op, Triton `_lookup`
   launch) for each table, `_cache_done.record`, and finally
   `epochs[0].fill_(epoch)` on the side stream.

So after the forward is running, the flag it spins on still depends on
(a) disk latency, (b) the side thread reacquiring the GIL, and (c) several
CUDA driver calls from that thread (kernel launches, event record). Any of
(b) or (c) that cannot proceed while a kernel spins turns into a wait that
only the 5 s timeout breaks. Two concrete ways (c) can block:
- Module loading. PyTorch sets `CUDA_MODULE_LOADING=LAZY` when unset
  (torch/cuda/__init__.py), the launch contract does not set it, and Triton
  loads each compiled kernel's module on its first launch. NVIDIA documents
  that loading may require context synchronization, and names exactly this
  pattern (a kernel spinning until another, not yet loaded, kernel runs) as a
  deadlock risk. It happens once per kernel, which matches the first-use-only
  pattern. The device-side spin makes the forward itself the thing the load
  waits for, so both Engram layers time out in turn: one 5 s plateau when it
  hits a prefill, two (10 s) in a verify graph, as observed.
- A GIL-holding blocking call on the main thread (a native extension call
  that waits for GPU work without releasing the GIL) would starve the Python
  segment after the read in the same way. Not identified in the pinned code.

A pure-I/O alternative is also consistent with the plateaus: with async
scheduling, job N+1's host read cannot start until `_ids_ready`, which is
ordered after forward N on the main stream, while forward N+1's layer-1 wait
starts right after forward N. Decode steps therefore expose the whole read
latency to the wait, and a cold read longer than 5 s times out with no
deadlock at all. The staggered per-rank NVMe bursts on first requests fit this
too. The two are not distinguishable from existing receipts.

Discriminator (highest yield): timestamp the side thread. In `lookup()` log
monotonic times for: start, after `_ids_ready.synchronize`, after
`ple_reader_run`, after each `lookup_op` returns, after the epoch
`fill_` returns, plus the forward launch time; and arm
`faulthandler.dump_traceback_later(3, repeat=True)` only while a job is
outstanding so a stack of every thread is written during a stall. Reading:
- read itself > 5 s: I/O latency;
- read finished, thread stuck in `lookup_op` or `fill_` (driver call): module
  load or other driver serialization;
- read finished, thread stuck at a Python line with no native frame: GIL.
Environment-only splits, one boot each: `CUDA_MODULE_LOADING=EAGER` (removes
CUDA lazy loading; Triton's own first-launch load remains, so a negative is
not conclusive); `VLLM_DS41_ENGRAM_OVERLAP=0` (removes the spin; the first
request must then match, slower).

Overlap-preserving repair (independent of which of the three dominates):
move every GIL and CUDA-API step off the post-launch critical path.
1. In `prepare_disk_engram`, on the main thread and before the forward
   launch, enqueue on the side stream: the ids D2H copy, a small wait kernel
   that spins on a mapped-host "rows ready" word (system-scope acquire
   loads), the `_lookup` kernels, and the epoch publish.
2. The host thread then only does GIL-free native work: wait on the ids
   event, `ple_reader_run`, and a release store to the mapped word (after the
   row writes to the mapped staging). No Python and no CUDA call remain between
   forward launch and readiness, so the forward's wait can only be waiting on
   real I/O.
3. Launch each involved kernel once during warmup so no module load happens
   while serving.
4. Keep a bound on the wait, but make expiry fail the step at the step's
   existing sync point (epoch-tagged failure word, propagated before outputs
   leave the engine), and propagate lookup exceptions in the same step.
If the discriminator shows pure I/O, items 1-3 still remove the hazard and
item 4 turns an over-long cold read into a visible error instead of wrong
output; a correct but slow path then needs either a longer bound for cold
reads or the synchronous fallback (`VLLM_DS41_ENGRAM_OVERLAP=0`) for the
first lookup per table. As an immediate, correctness-preserving mitigation
for qualification, `VLLM_DS41_ENGRAM_OVERLAP=0` removes the spin at the cost of
exposing lookup latency.

## Engram host-path timing diagnostic (prepared 2026-09-25, NOT applied, not built)

Purpose: the next diagnostic only if the first-use residual reproduces on the
ring-fix candidate e7b273407022. It timestamps every host boundary between
`prepare_disk_engram` and the side-thread epoch publication, so a 5 s or 10 s
plateau can be attributed to one segment. Diagnostic only: no repair.

Files, all `claude_*` or `claude-engram-timing*` in this tree:

| File | Role |
| --- | --- |
| claude_engram_timing.py | helper; becomes vllm/models/deepseek_v4_1/claude_engram_timing.py |
| claude_prepare_engram_timing.py | composes patched files from pinned blobs via git show |
| claude-engram-timing.patch | unified diff of all edits plus the new helper |
| claude-engram-timing-{model,_impl,disk_table}.py | patched copies |
| claude-engram-timing.lock.json | sha256 e737a22db6226ab6... (wording revision); input/output digests, delivery mode |
| claude_install_engram_timing.py | in-image installer; confines changes |
| Dockerfile.claude-engram-timing, claude-engram-timing.ignore | build recipe over e7b273407022 |
| claude_test_engram_timing.py | 18 stdlib tests |

Pins: vLLM 1794dcf1 and b12x a7d7d29b. SM121 is capability family 120, so
DS4.1 loads `deepseek_v4_1` (not `deepseek_v41`). The runtime.lock.json
"files" digests equal the pinned blobs for all three targets.

Delivery split, and why. Every B12X CuTe disk-cache key includes a hash of
every file under the b12x package (`_b12x_package_fingerprint`). Editing
`_impl.py` or `disk_table.py` in place would therefore cold-compile every
B12X kernel on first boot and change the very first-use conditions under
test. So only vLLM files change on disk: model.py is replaced and the helper
is added. At model import the helper verifies that the live B12X files are
the pinned inputs and that the patched copies match the lock. It then
compiles only the four edited functions from the shipped patched copies,
keeping their line numbers. It binds them over `_impl.run_lookups`,
`_State.run_lookup`, `DiskRowCache._stage_ids` and `_read_staged`, plus the
`api` and package re-exports. Any mismatch fails at import. B12X, Triton,
CuTe and XDG caches stay inherited from the ring-fix image. vLLM and
Inductor caches go to a separate TIMING_CACHE directory, because the
model.py edit changes vLLM compile keys.

Events, in order per step:

- **Main thread:** prepare-begin (tokens, last_epoch), finish-wait-begin/end
  around the previous job's `result()`, prepare-prev-job-done,
  prepare-hash-launched, prepare-staged, job-begin (arms the watchdog),
  prepare-dispatched, prepare-end.
- **ds41-engram thread:** lookup-begin, lookup-device-set,
  lookup-ready-wait-enqueued, run-lookups-begin, then txn-enter-begin and
  txn-entered for each table.
- **Staging and reads:** stage-copy-begin and stage-recorded per cache,
  ids-sync-begin/end and read-returned (or gds-read-returned), on the
  lookup thread for table 0 and on b12x-engram-read_N for table 1.
- **Tail:** reads-primary-returned, reads-joined, lookup-op-begin,
  kernel-launch-begin/end, lookup-op-end per table, run-lookups-end,
  lookup-run-lookups-returned, lookup-epoch-fill-enqueued, job-end.

Line format: monotonic_ns, node, pid, native tid, thread name, the
outstanding job epoch, the event, then key=value pairs. All lines go to
/cache/ds41-engram-timing/<node>-<pid>.log, which is
~/.cache/vllm-jj-ds41-tp4/ds41-engram-timing on the host.

Enable at runtime, no restart: create /cache/ds41-engram-timing.enable.
Put the word "stacks" in it to also arm faulthandler. The file is
re-checked at most once per second. The image changes nothing else
while the file is absent.

Guarantees, tested against pinned code on fakes:

- Pinned and timed B12X functions make identical calls in identical order,
  with timing disabled and enabled.
- This holds for the serial path, the error path and both GDS branches.
- Stripped of `_cet` lines and the one try/finally, each patched file equals
  its pinned source, ignoring whitespace.
- No event argument calls synchronize, wait, result, item, cpu, tolist or
  torch.
- `job_end` runs in `finally`.
- The installer passes on a simulated pinned tree and refuses both a rerun
  and a drifted B12X file.

Limits:

- **Perturbation (corrected 2026-09-25 on user review).** Each enabled
  event is Python formatting plus one os.write, about 25 per step across
  three threads. The first enabled event also creates the log directory and
  opens the log, on the path under test. The cost is unmeasured, so no
  per-event figure is claimed. Small changes in interleaving and GIL
  hand-off can decide whether a stall crosses the fixed 5000 ms wait, so
  timing can make a timeout disappear or appear. A non-reproduction with
  timing on is not evidence the stall is gone. Disabled, each call site
  still evaluates its arguments, reads the clock and checks a cached flag.
  The enable file is opened at most once per second.
- **What faulthandler shows.** It runs from a C watchdog thread and prints
  only the Python frames of each thread. A thread blocked in a native call
  and a thread waiting for the GIL on the same line look identical. The
  timestamps do not separate them either, because the event after a native
  call runs only after the GIL is reacquired.
- **faulthandler safety.** Dumps read frames without the GIL. CPython
  documents them as best effort. That is why "stacks" is a separate
  opt-in. There is no conflict with vLLM's B12X_HANG_DUMP registration,
  which uses signal registration, not the watchdog timer.
- **Kernel timing.** kernel-launch-end is the host-side end of the launch
  call, not the GPU completion. The GPU-side 5000 ms wait in
  `_wait_engram_rows` is not timed. The time the forward waits for the
  epoch is only inferred, from prepare-end versus
  lookup-epoch-fill-enqueued.
- **Import timing.** `install_b12x` imports b12x.sequence.engram.api when
  model.py is imported, instead of on first use.
- **Launch.** Launching needs a candidate amendment for the new image,
  through the user's freeze tooling, which was not touched.

### Wording revision and native lazy-load coverage (2026-09-25, user review)

Context: on the ring-fix image, the 128 and 16K cold requests are bitwise
identical across six runs. Two meadow385 to filler385 transition cycles now
pass bitwise. The first 385 request still differs from repeats 1 to 5, all
outputs correct. That first-385 difference is the residual this kit would
target.

Wording corrections, no code change:

- **Stall claim withdrawn.** The claim that instrumentation "cannot hide or
  create a multi-second stall" is withdrawn. The forward waits on a fixed
  5000 ms timeout, so a small scheduling change can decide the outcome.
- **Cost figures removed.** The per-event microsecond estimate was never
  measured and is removed.
- **faulthandler claim corrected.** The earlier text said timestamps
  separate a GIL wait from native blocking. They do not: an event after a
  native call runs only after the GIL is reacquired.
- **Digests.** The patched model.py, `_impl` and `disk_table` copies kept
  the same digests (4313ab2b..., f9f4e380..., 4a5c2ef7...). The helper,
  patch and lock changed. Tests passed 18/18, and the installer simulation
  passed.

Lazy-load coverage, from pinned source; the image's Triton was not inspected.
Every lookup-thread call that could first-use load something sits inside
its own pair of events:

| Segment | Candidate first-use cost |
| --- | --- |
| lookup-begin to lookup-device-set | first CUDA use from the new ds41-engram thread |
| lookup-device-set to lookup-ready-wait-enqueued | stream context, event wait |
| txn-enter-begin to txn-entered | cache lock, device guard, capture check, wait on the previous cache_done |
| stage-copy-begin to stage-recorded | async D2H into pinned host, event record |
| ids-sync-begin to ids-sync-end | host wait for all GPU work before the ready event |
| ids-sync-end to read-returned | io_uring reads (cold page cache, first reader use) |
| lookup-op-begin to kernel-launch-begin | custom-op dispatch, plan lookup |
| kernel-launch-begin to kernel-launch-end | first launch of the warmup-compiled `_lookup` |
| lookup-run-lookups-returned to lookup-epoch-fill-enqueued | epoch fill_ launch |

The `_lookup` kernel is built by `warmup` in the compile pool, so its
module is expected to load on first launch in the serving process.
`prepared_tokens` is do_not_specialize, so a new token count should not
recompile. Both points are expected from source, not observed.

The kit localizes a stall to a segment. It does not identify the cause,
for these reasons:

1. **GIL.** Each segment includes GIL reacquisition. A main thread holding
   the GIL, for example during Triton compilation or a first-use load
   inside a non-releasing call, inflates lookup-thread segments while
   looking like native time.
2. **ids-sync.** This segment includes every GPU operation queued before
   the ready event. GPU backlog and a lazy load elsewhere look the same.
3. **Cross-thread driver locks.** A lazy module load on the main thread
   can block the lookup thread's CUDA calls. The main thread is timed only
   up to prepare-end, not through the forward.
4. **Stacks.** Dumps appear only for jobs outstanding at least 3 s, and
   they have no native frames.
5. **Measurement first use.** Opening the log directory and file is new
   first-use work on the same path.

Discriminators that need no kit change:

- **Paired arms.** Run the timing image with the enable file present and
  absent under otherwise identical conditions.
- **EAGER.** An arm with `CUDA_MODULE_LOADING=EAGER`, with timing on.
- **No overlap.** An arm with `VLLM_DS41_ENGRAM_OVERLAP=0`.
- **Warm-up.** A warm-up request of a different length before the first
  385 request.

If segments stay ambiguous, candidate additions for review are:

- `time.thread_time_ns()` per event, which separates CPU-bound work such as
  module load or compilation from blocked time such as I/O, sync or GIL;
- opening the log at install, which removes the measurement's own
  first-use work.

Neither is implemented.

- **Log-name collisions across boots.** Container pids repeat across boots
  (Worker pids 453, 456 and 533 seen), and the helper appends to
  `<node>-<pid>.log`. A second run on the same node can append into the
  first run's files. The flag-absence check does not cover the log
  directory. Suggest also requiring that ds41-engram-timing is absent, or
  moving it aside after collection.
- **Cold vLLM and Inductor cache.** TIMING_CACHE is new, so the first boot
  compiles vLLM and Inductor from scratch. B12X and Triton caches are
  inherited. Record per node whether the cache directory existed before
  boot, and whether it did for the ring-fix reference run, before calling
  the runs matched.
- **Stacks mode perturbs more than events.** From my reading of CPython's
  faulthandler (not checked against the image's 3.12.3), each
  `dump_traceback_later` starts a watchdog OS thread. Each cancel waits
  for that thread to exit, with the GIL held. So stacks mode adds a thread
  start per job on the main thread and a thread join per job on the lookup
  thread. If the residual disappears with stacks on, repeat with timing
  on and stacks off before concluding anything.
- **Binding gate coverage.** The gate imports the helper and calls
  `install_b12x`, but does not import the patched model.py, which calls
  install at import time. A fault there would surface only at boot, and
  would fail closed.
- **Expected outcome exits 1.** A reproduced first-385 difference makes
  probe_repeatability exit 1. start_moe_repaired then raises, and
  run_engram_timing exits 1 after collecting logs.
- **Flag present from boot.** The helper therefore opens its log and
  stacks files during startup Engram jobs, if any run. That keeps the
  measurement's own first-use file work off the first request. Startup
  events will also show whether warmup exercises the disk-Engram path.

Checked and fine:

- **Mount and node identity.** The flag path matches the /cache mount, and
  DS41_NODE is set.
- **Enable content.** The enable file contains "stacks".
- **Distribution naming.** Receipt and tag names line up for the
  engram-timing arm.
- **Freeze.** It pins the ring-fix parent and inherited labels, and
  run_node checks both diagnostic labels.
- **Collection timing.** Containers keep running through collection. The
  helper stops writing within about a second of the flag move.
- **Request order.** The 128, 385 and 16K order matches the ring-fix run.

First-385 evidence, from ring-fix run 20260925T163121Z (read-only):

| Request | Prefill s | Token-0 top alternatives |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 385 first | 7.005 | 510 at -14.750, 738 at -15.750 |
| 385 repeats 1 to 5 | 0.44 to 0.48 | 738 at -12.625, The at -13.000 |
| 128 first, then 2nd, 3rd | 14.97, 3.08, 0.95 | identical across all six |
| 16K first, then 2nd | 6.05, 4.01 | identical across all six |

- **Size of the shift.** Alternative logits move by about 2 nats and the
  runner-up token changes. That is larger than typical reduction-order
  noise, and suggests different inputs to some layer. This is not proven.
- **Where the time goes.** The whole first-385 excess is in prefill. Queue
  time and decode are unchanged.
- **Counters.** Metric counter deltas are identical between the first
  request and its repeats: tokens, spec decode and preemptions.
- **Disk.** NVMe read volume per request window, at one-second sampling,
  is similar for the first 385 and its repeats. The Engram reader appears
  to hit disk on every request, so cold-read volume does not distinguish
  them.
- **JIT monitor.** The vLLM monitor is active in warn mode and logged
  nothing during requests on any rank. That excludes, if its hooks work,
  CuTe DSL compiles, Triton JIT and autotune, and TileLang compiles. It
  does not cover B12X or Triton disk-cache loads, lazy CUDA module loads,
  FlashInfer JIT or first-touch effects.
- **Warmup coverage.** FlashInfer autotune buckets include 128, 256 and
  512. 16K is the maximum batched-token size. 385 is plausibly the first
  prefill shape not exercised during warmup. That is an inference, not
  verified.

Open hypotheses; none is established:

- **H1, Engram publication timeout in the first-385 prefill.** It fits an
  excess above 5 s confined to prefill, and a material logit shift. The
  timing kit and an overlap=0 arm test it directly. It needs an
  explanation for why only this request's lookup would lag. A GIL-heavy
  first-use cost on the main thread, after layer 1's wait was queued, is
  one candidate. The first 128 request's long prefill without divergence
  fits if that stall came before the wait was queued.
- **H2, first execution of a new shape through an unmonitored path.** An
  object-cache load, plan or workspace setup, or lazy module load normally
  changes only latency. It changes numerics only if the first call takes a
  different path or reads state that is initialised lazily.
- **H3, never-written versus written state.** Transition cycles show that
  prior content no longer matters. That does not exclude a difference
  between never-written and ever-written memory, for example fresh blocks
  or slots, or a partial compressor group at odd length. The fixed ring
  bug had this same first-versus-repeat signature.
- **H4, rows read from disk versus rows from a row cache.** It applies if
  the two paths can yield different bytes for the same hash.

Separating arms, one boot each, request order otherwise unchanged:

| Arm | Before the measured 385 | Shape | Engram rows | Blocks |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| a | same content, different length (for example 386) | cold | warm | written |
| b | different content, same 385 length | warm | cold | written |
| c | no warm-up (current control) | cold | cold | written by 128s |
| d | overlap=0, no warm-up | cold | cold | written by 128s |

- **a fixes it:** points to H1 or H4.
- **b fixes it:** points to H2 or H3.
- **d fixes it:** implicates publication timing.
- **Kit output:** the timing run shows which rank's job, if any, stayed
  outstanding past about 5 s in the first-385 prefill, and what the main
  thread was doing at 3 s.

## Engram timing result: first-385 blocked lookup launch (analysis 2026-09-25)

Evidence: receipts/engram-host-timing-20260925T170403Z (four rank logs and
stacks) and engram-timing-20260925T170409Z. Read-only analysis.

### What the four ranks show

Job 384 is the first 385-token prefill. On all ranks, the host call that
launches table 0's `_lookup` blocked, then the table 1 launch returned at
once. The epoch was published only after that.

| Rank | ids-sync end, ms | launch 1, ms | launch 2, ms | launch end minus (ids-sync end + 5000), ms |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| dusty | 199.4 | 5025.7 | 0.08 | 28.2 |
| toby | 231.2 | 5018.0 | 0.14 | 28.7 |
| rusty | 227.9 | 5016.9 | 0.09 | 28.1 |
| kirby | 192.3 | 5024.0 | 0.07 | 28.9 |

- **Release is tied to GPU progress.** Anchored on the moment the GPU
  passed the ready event, the release spread is 0.8 ms. Anchored on the
  launch call, the spread is 9 ms. The release therefore tracks a GPU
  event about 5028 ms after ready, which is where the layer-1 spin started
  plus its 5000 ms timeout. It does not track an independent host timer.
  The 5 s duration alone is not the argument.
- **One timeout, not two.** The timing run's first 385 took 5.95 s against
  0.37 s for repeats. So layer 14's wait was satisfied, and only layer 1
  consumed unpublished rows. All ranks timed out, so the all-reduced
  staged rows were wrong on every rank.
- **The diverged values vary by boot.** Token-0 alternatives differ from
  the ring-fix boot's first 385 (738 at -14.125 here, 510 at -14.750
  there). Repeats are identical in both runs. That fits stale or partly
  written staged rows being consumed.
- **Why the first request, from timing.** The ids-sync wait was 190 to 230
  ms, so the lookup launch came 200 to 240 ms after prepare-end. Repeats
  launch 3 to 15 ms after. The first 8192 chunk also had a long ids-sync
  (up to 351 ms) but launched in 0.2 ms, so a late launch alone does not
  block.
- **Stack dump taken 3 s into job 384, identical on all four ranks:**
  - the lookup thread was in Triton `backends/nvidia/driver.py` line 331
    `__call__`, reached from `compiler.py` line 512 `runner`, from
    `_State.run_lookup`;
  - the worker main thread was in the eager prefill forward, inside
    b12x `bf16_gemv/_preparation.py` line 71, which is
    `torch.mm(x, weight.T, out=out)`. That is the torch backend of a
    projection plan, called from attention `_prepare_global_kv`.
- **Not resolved:**
  - Which statement line 331 is. The image's Triton (NGC PyTorch 26.08)
    was not readable without a node, so it could be the native launch or
    a launcher prelude such as scratch allocation.
  - Which driver-level wait both threads were in. Candidates are an
    implicit device synchronization in a lazily loaded kernel or module,
    an allocator free or malloc path, cuBLAS first-use setup for a new M,
    or launch-queue capacity.
  - Nothing here distinguishes them. The launch environment sets neither
    CUDA_MODULE_LOADING, which torch defaults to LAZY, nor
    CUDA_DEVICE_MAX_CONNECTIONS.
  - Cheap discriminators: read the image's driver.py around line 331; a
    CUDA_MODULE_LOADING=EAGER arm; a native stack of the worker during the
    stall; torch.cuda.memory_stats deltas around the first 385.

### A second, different signature (warmup, not requests)

Jobs 361 and 362 are startup warmup steps with 10 and 2 rows. Each shows
about 10 s, which is two spin timeouts. The lookup thread and read-pool
thread were both in `Event.synchronize` on ids-ready, while the worker main
thread was in `_finish_engram_job`, not in a CUDA call.

So the side stream's device-to-host ids copy did not complete until both
layer waits timed out. That is a GPU-side ordering dependency, not a
blocked host call. The mechanism is unidentified. Hardware-queue
head-of-line blocking behind a forward submitted earlier is one
possibility.

Job 368, during the first 128 request, stalled 3.2 s the same way, with
the main thread idle in zmq poll.

Consequence: `failed` is written as the constant 1 and never reset, so it
has been set since warmup. Any fail-closed check must not reuse it as is.

### Defect class, from pinned source

The forward's `_wait_engram_rows` (common/engram.py:620, called at :901
with 5000 ms) spins on the GPU for an epoch. That epoch is published only
after the side thread makes further host CUDA calls: an event
synchronize, two custom-op `_lookup` launches, the epoch `fill_`, and the
transaction's event record.

Any host-side wait for device progress on that path can deadlock against
the spin until it times out. That covers a context-wide implicit sync, a
lock held during one, queue capacity, and submission order. On timeout the
kernel writes `failed=1` and proceeds, and nothing reads `failed`. The
silent wrong-row consumption, not the stall, is the correctness defect.

### Proposed repair, not implemented

It keeps overlap and fails closed. It does not depend on which host
blocker it was.

1. **Submit every GPU operation of a job before the forward.** In
   `_start_engram_job`, on the main thread and before it returns, enqueue
   on the side stream:
   - wait on `ready`;
   - per table, the cache-done wait, the ids device-to-host copy, and a
     stream write of the epoch into a mapped ids-ready word;
   - a new single-CTA gate kernel that polls a mapped host-release word
     for the epoch or a failure code, with its own timeout;
   - the two unchanged `_lookup` launches;
   - a tiny publish kernel replacing `fill_`, which writes the epoch into
     `ready` only if the gate succeeded, and otherwise writes the epoch
     into a failure word so the consumer stops spinning;
   - the cache-done record.
2. **The side thread then makes no CUDA calls.** It polls the mapped
   ids-ready word, runs the native io_uring reads (the non-GDS path writes
   directly into the mapped write-combined weight and scale caches that
   `_lookup` reads), then does a release store of the epoch into the
   host-release word. On a reader exception it stores a failure code.
   - A native release store is needed. A Python store gives no ordering
     guarantee on aarch64.
   - The gate needs a system-scope acquire before `_lookup` reads rows.
   - The GDS path uses the stream and needs separate treatment.
3. **Fail closed.**
   - The consumer writes the target epoch, not 1, into `failed`, and
     also exits on the gate's failure code.
   - Once per step after the forward, a one-word MAX all-reduce over TP
     makes a timeout on any rank visible to all. Rows are all-reduced, so
     one rank's timeout corrupts every rank. Its cost is unmeasured.
   - Copy that word with the step's outputs on the existing copy stream.
     In `get_output` (vllm/v1/worker/gpu/async_utils.py), after the
     existing `copy_event.synchronize()`, raise when it equals the step's
     epoch. That mirrors the EP all2all `_has_fault` check at the same
     place. No new host synchronization is added.

Scope:

- **B12X changes.** Step 1 needs a DiskRowCache API that splits enqueue
  from the host read across threads; the transaction currently asserts a
  single owning thread. Step 2 needs a native publish helper. Both change
  b12x bytes, so they cost one full B12X recompile.
- **vLLM changes.** model.py, common/engram.py for the gate, publish and
  failure semantics, and async_utils.py.
- **A staged, vLLM-only first step.** Step 3 alone (epoch-valued
  `failed`, the one-word reduce, the `get_output` raise) stops silent
  corruption, at the price of an engine error when a stall happens.
- **What it does not explain.** It does not identify the host-side
  blocker. It should make the path immune to all the candidates listed.
  Whether it also removes the warmup signature depends on that
  mechanism, which is still open.

## Engram fail-closed repair: proposed patch (2026-09-25, for review, not built)

User supplied: image `driver.py` line 331 is the native `self.launch` call,
so the first-385 block was inside the launch itself.

Scope: fail closed on an Engram publication timeout, per step, across TP.
It does not remove the stall. Overlap, the 5000 ms timeout and B12X are
unchanged. The patch is composed from pinned 1794dcf1 blobs, which equal
runtime.lock.json "files" and so the ring-fix image.

### Seam assessment

- **No new collective.** Each Engram layer already all-reduces its staged
  rows over TP. One leading row carries each rank's timeout flag through
  that same reduction, so every rank sees the sum.
- **Why the sum is safe for the rows.** `_lookup` writes zeros outside its
  shard, so each staged element has at most one nonzero contributor.
  Sums are therefore exact and independent of reduction order and
  chunking. The extra row cannot change row bits. This is shown by CPU
  test and source argument, not verified on GPU.
- **Output seam.** It mirrors the EP all2all fault: a device value is
  copied on the existing output-copy stream, and `get_output` raises after
  the existing `copy_event.synchronize()`. No new synchronization.
- **Rejected: poisoned logits.** It needs `VLLM_RAISE_ON_LOGIT_NANS=1`, a
  launch-contract change, adds a per-step NaN count over the full
  vocabulary, and gives a misleading error.
- **Alternative: a worker wrapper.** A wrapper like the RoCEnante fail-stop
  wrapper could read a mapped-memory fault word after `get_output`. That
  needs host epoch bookkeeping and mapped-memory visibility, is about the
  same size, and was not chosen.

### Mechanism

1. **Flag row.** Engram allocates `engram_io_rows` with max_tokens+1 rows.
   Row 0 is the flag, and `staged_rows = engram_io_rows[1:]` is the only
   thing lookups and zeroing bind. A late lookup therefore cannot write
   the flag.
2. **Wait kernel.** `_wait_engram_rows` gains a `flag` argument and always
   stores `timed_out` (0 or 1) as bf16 into io[0,0]. That removes stale
   flags, including the sum an in-place reduce leaves behind.
3. **Forward.** With overlap on, the forward runs: alias check (host), wait,
   `all_reduce(io[:n+1])`, then `_record_engram_fault(reduced,
   expected, fault_epoch)`, which folds a nonzero or NaN sum into a
   monotonic per-model fault epoch. `rows = reduced[1:]` then feeds wkv.
   The non-overlap path is byte-for-byte the original expression.
4. **Snapshot.** DeepseekV4Model allocates `_engram_fault_epoch`, only with
   overlap, and shares it with its Engram layers. `snapshot_engram_fault()`
   launches `_snapshot_engram_fault`, which writes int64
   [failed, fault_epoch, expected] where failed means fault >= expected
   and fault > 0.
5. **Runner hook.** The model state's `step_fault_snapshot()` is called by
   the runner's duck-typed `_model_step_fault()` on `main_stream`, after
   sampling and before the next prepare. Both `AsyncOutput` sites pass it
   as `model_fault`.
6. **Output check.** `_check_model_fault` raises on failed. An older fault
   seen without failed can only come from a step whose output was never
   materialized (warmup or dummy); it is logged once, not raised.

### Tested source mechanics

13 stdlib tests plus 9 CPU-torch tests. The torch tests use the task-local
.venv-snapshot-cpu and run the real patched Python on four simulated ranks,
with Triton kernels and the all-reduce emulated.

- **Reproducibility.** Output is reproduced from the pinned blobs.
- **Exact removed-line sets per file.** Only the intended lines change.
- **Decorators and signatures unchanged.** This test caught a real bug:
  the first anchor moved `@torch.inference_mode()` and `@step_eplb_after()`
  off `sample_tokens`.
- **Order of the new calls.** Wait, reduce, record, then `rows=reduced[1:]`.
- **Both output sites.** Each passes the snapshot, and the check runs right
  after the existing sync.
- **Healthy steps.** The rows seen by wkv equal the original sums bitwise.
- **Detection across ranks.** A timeout on rank 2 alone makes every rank
  report failed with the step epoch, and the check raises.
- **No stale positives.** A warmup fault, with in-place or out-of-place
  reduce, gives no false positive on later steps and one warning.
- **Flag isolation.** A late lookup cannot touch the flag.
- **Fail-closed guards.** An alias break or a missing fault buffer raises.
- **Ordering assumption.** A snapshot taken after the next prepare misses
  the fault, which documents why the snapshot position matters.
- **Exactness.** Single-owner sums are order independent, and a
  two-contributor counterexample shows why that property matters.

### Not verified (GPU or deployment)

- **Triton compilation and behaviour.** Whether Triton accepts the
  `timed_out` loop-carried variable (the same idiom as the existing
  `waiting = 0`), the bf16 flag store, and the int64 compare and max in
  the new kernels.
- **CUDA graph capture.** FULL graphs up to 32 tokens contain the wait,
  reduce and record kernels. Capture-time pointer stability for `io` and
  the reduced tensor is expected but not verified.
- **All-reduce paths.** RoCEnante (up to 2 MB) and NCCL, with the
  one-row-larger payload. At n=170 tokens the payload crosses 2 MB, so
  that size moves from RoCEnante to NCCL. Numerics are exact by the
  argument above, but the path and timing change.
- **Throughput.** One extra row per reduce, one tiny kernel per Engram
  layer, one per step, and a 24-byte copy per step. Cost is unmeasured.
- **Error propagation.** That a raise in `get_output` on the async output
  thread stops the engine as the EP and RoCEnante faults do.
- **Runner order.** That the runner keeps calling sample_tokens(k) before
  execute_model(k+1) (source-derived).
- **Whole-model behaviour.** Numerics are identical to the ring-fix
  candidate on real inputs only by argument, pending a GPU A/B.

### Design limits

- **Fail-stop, not recovery.** The faulted step still executes. Its
  outputs and any later ones are withheld because get_output raises.
- **Warmup faults are not fatal.** The observed 10 s warmup stalls would
  only log. Making them fatal would fail startup at every boot showing
  that signature.
- **The late lookup of a faulted step still races later GPU work.** That
  is acceptable only because the engine stops.
- **The legacy `failed` word still sticks at 1.** It is left untouched and
  unused.
- **Not composable with the timing image as is.** Both edit model.py.
- **Generic runner files change.** `async_utils.py` and `model_runner.py`
  change, as duck-typed no-ops for other models.

### Revision 2 of the fail-closed proposal (2026-09-25, after user review)

The lock is now 4693deea006f5868... Tests: 14 stdlib and 11 CPU-torch, all
passing.

1. **Compile blocker fixed.** The user found that `data_ptr()` in
   forward fails Dynamo fullgraph with `DataPtrVariable`. The alias check
   is now `Engram._check_fault_io_alias()`, called first in
   `invalidate_disk_output()`, which is the eager seam that
   `prepare_disk`, `stage_disk`, `prepare_dummy_output` and
   `prepare_disk_engram` all pass through. Both model entry points refuse
   to run under compile or capture. Forward contains no `data_ptr`.
   - A new test compiles the real patched forward with
     `torch.compile(fullgraph=True, backend="eager")`, in healthy and
     timeout cases.
   - A negative control reproduces the `DataPtrVariable` failure for the
     old placement.
2. **Fewer Triton edits.** The record and snapshot Triton kernels are
   replaced by torch ops, `where`, `maximum` and `copy_` in forward and
   `torch.cat` for the snapshot. The only Triton edit left is the flag
   store in `_wait_engram_rows`.
3. **Strict semantics.** The fault epoch is monotonic and never reset.
   `get_output` raises whenever fault_epoch > 0, and the message says
   "this step" or "an earlier step". There is no warning path. A fault in
   any step whose output is never checked (warmup, dummy, or a
   no-sample path) fails the next checked output.
   - Property: once a boot emits a successful output, no Engram
     timeout has occurred on any rank since process start, up to that
     step's snapshot.
   - Qualification should still end with a final checked request, so a
     timeout after the last suite request cannot go unobserved.

### Runner trace at 1794dcf1 (source only)

- **Output path.** `multiproc_executor._execute_worker_rpc` calls
  `handle_output` only on `output_rank`. `enqueue_output` calls
  `AsyncModelRunnerOutput.get_output()` for every output it receives, and
  an exception becomes a FAILURE response.
  - Non-output ranks never call `get_output`, so cross-rank propagation
    must be carried in-band, which the row reduction does.
- **Every sampled step creates an `AsyncOutput` at one of two sites.**
  This includes chunked prefill, the async look-ahead step, the
  speculative verify and the boundary-checkpoint resume. The snapshot is
  enqueued on `main_stream` after sampling, so it follows that step's
  record ops in stream order.
- **Paths without output.**
  - `kv_connector.no_forward` early returns run no forward and no Engram
    job.
  - The boundary-checkpoint resume returns before
    `model_state.prepare_inputs`, so it runs no Engram job, but its
    later `sample_tokens` still snapshots and checks.
  - `sample_tokens` returns None when `execute_model_state` is None,
    which follows a prior failure.
  - `_dummy_run` and warmup call `sample_tokens` but never `get_output`.
    Under strict semantics their faults surface at the next checked
    output.
- **Epoch advances.** Only `_start_engram_job` (real steps) and
  `prepare_dummy_engram` (dummy steps) advance `_engram_epoch`. The DSpark
  draft model and the VL wrapper contain no Engram, so draft steps
  advance nothing. Detection no longer depends on epoch equality, so an
  interleaved dummy advance cannot hide a fault.

### Progress repair: review of ENGRAM-PROGRESS-REPAIR.md

Both candidates meet the key property that ids copy, reads, lookups and
publication are submitted before the forward. The job-384 evidence adds
one requirement: the worker main thread was blocked for about 5 s inside
`torch.mm` (cuBLAS). PyTorch ops generally hold the GIL, so any producer
that needs the GIL (the current Python lookup thread, or Candidate A with
a Python worker) can be starved by the same event. The CPU producer must
therefore be native and GIL-free.

**Candidate A, mapped words and a GPU gate.** With a native pthread worker
it depends only on memory stores and so is independent of driver locks.
Costs:

- a second spinning kernel, the gate, which relies on concurrent
  residency;
- CPU release and GPU system-scope acquire code on write-combined mapped
  memory;
- ids readiness written by the GPU and polled by the CPU;
- gate timeout and failure encoding;
- a transaction API split across threads.

That is the most new concurrency code of the two.

**Candidate B, a native host function on the side stream.** The core at
`_ple_reader.c` lines 334 to 363 is already CUDA-free and Python-free; it
uses the reader mutex, `ple_plan`, and `ple_uring_run`, whose scatter is a
CPU memcpy into the mapped caches. It fits `cuLaunchHostFunc` directly.
Stream order gives ids-copy-before-read and read-before-lookup, and
FIFO use of one side stream replaces the cache-done events and host
ownership checks. No polling and no gate spin.

Open points for B:

- **Callback progress during the observed block.** Whether the driver
  runs host functions while another thread is blocked in the call seen at
  job 384. The EAGER discriminator result bears on this, and it must be
  tested.
- **Serialization.** Host functions may be serialized per context. Pinned
  B12X and vLLM launch none, but NCCL and CUDA internals were not checked.
- **Hangs and errors.** A hung disk read blocks the side stream with no
  cancel. The consumer timeout plus fail-closed still bounds output
  correctness. Callbacks are omitted on a context error.
- **Table overlap.** Sequential callbacks lose it; the logs show about
  2 ms per 385-token read.

**Recommendation: B as the smallest sound repair, keeping overlap.**

- **Native.**
  - Factor lines 336 to 361 into `ple_read_core()`, shared by
    `py_ple_reader_run`, so there is one implementation.
  - Add a `CUhostFn` that runs the core on a per-enqueue malloc'd job
    (reader, ids, weights, scales, count). It records failure natively
    with an atomic status and last message, issues a full fence before
    returning (write-combined stores), and frees the job.
  - Add a constructor that returns the (fn, job) addresses, and a free
    for the case where the launch itself fails.
- **B12X Python.** Add `DiskRowCache.enqueue_read(ids, count, stream)`,
  which does the device-to-host ids copy into `ids_host` and then
  `cuLaunchHostFunc` via `cuda.bindings`. Add a
  `run_lookups`-equivalent that enqueues both tables' reads and then the
  unchanged `_lookup` launches. Refuse the GDS path with overlap.
  `close()` synchronizes the side stream before releasing the reader or
  buffers.
- **vLLM.**
  - `_start_engram_job` enqueues on the main thread, before returning to
    launch the forward: wait(ready), the reads, the lookups, then
    publication.
  - Publication is a tiny kernel that writes the epoch only if the native
    status is OK. On a read error it does not publish, so the consumer
    times out and the flag fails the step closed. That is slow but
    correct, for a rare error.
  - Remove the ThreadPoolExecutor. `_finish_engram_job` only reports the
    previous native status and message.
  - Keep the consumer spin, the 5000 ms bound and this fail-closed patch.
- **Cost.** It changes B12X bytes (one B12X recompile) and the native
  extension.

**Acceptance evidence, beyond the document's list:**

- An injected host-thread block inside a CUDA call during the forward:
  hold the main thread in a synthetic driver-waiting call while the
  consumer spins, in eager and graph modes.
- An injected read failure and an injected slow read, each on one rank.
- A test that the reader mutex is never held by a thread waiting on the
  side stream.
- The whole-boot zero-timeout requirement: no raise from the fail-closed
  check at any point, including the final probe.

## Candidate B, B12X and native portion (2026-09-25, proposal, not built into any image)

Correction: my earlier claim that "PyTorch ops generally hold the GIL" was
too broad, and I withdraw it. The user's reproducer
(`receipts/cuda-producer-progress-20260925T174435Z`) shows the producer
thread was already inside its native launch during the main thread's cold
`torch.mm`. GIL starvation is not needed to explain the stall.

User GPU evidence that motivates B:

- **Launch stalls.** A cold `torch.mm` 385x512x128 stalls a producer launch
  until a 1 s consumer timeout, in 3 of 3 runs. Prewarmed or no mm gives 0
  of 3, and a prequeued GPU producer gives 0 of 3.
- **Host-function smoke.** A native `cudaLaunchHostFunc` callback passes 3
  of 3. In `receipts/cuda-producer-progress-20260925T174915Z` it wrote 8192
  bytes through a real B12X MappedHostAllocation write-combined alias, and
  a prequeued GPU consumer read the exact checksum with no timeout.

Artifacts, all in this directory:

| File | Role |
| --- | --- |
| claude_prepare_ple_batch.py | composes from pinned a7d7d29b blobs and checks runtime.lock digests |
| claude-ple-batch.patch | the diff, 544 added lines |
| claude-ple-batch.lock.json | sha256 7af8633923e20a43...; input and output digests |
| claude-ple-batch-{_ple_reader.c,_storage.c,disk_table.py,_impl.py,api.py,__init__.py} | patched copies |
| claude_test_ple_batch_native.py | 13 tests; builds pinned, patched-test and patched-production extensions |
| claude_test_ple_batch_python.py | 11 stdlib tests with fakes |

### Exported API

The native module `_b12x_loader_storage` keeps `ABI_VERSION` at 1; the
functions are additive:

```
ple_batch(readers, ids, weights, scales, counts, status) -> handle
ple_batch_launch(handle, stream_handle) -> None
ple_batch_result(handle, timeout_s) -> (state, message | None)
```

- **`ple_batch`.** Every argument except `status` is a tuple with one entry
  per table (1 to 8 tables, distinct readers). `status` is a writable,
  8-byte-aligned int64 buffer. The same size and overlap checks as
  `ple_reader_run` apply, plus no overlap with `status`. It retains the
  reader capsules and buffer exports.
- **`ple_batch_launch`.** Enqueues the callback with `cudaLaunchHostFunc`,
  with the GIL released. If the launch fails, the state becomes
  launch_failed, every reference is released at once, and RuntimeError is
  raised.
- **`ple_batch_result`.** A negative timeout waits, 0 polls, and a positive
  value is a bounded wait; waits release the GIL. The state is prepared,
  pending, done or launch_failed. References are released once the job is
  done.

B12X Python:

- **`launch_batch_read(caches, counts, status_host) -> DiskBatchJob`**, in
  `disk_table.py`.
  - Requires: every cache in a transaction on this thread and one stream,
    a count equal to what `_stage_ids` staged, distinct caches, the
    io_uring backend, and `status_host` a contiguous CPU int64 of one
    element.
  - GDS raises NotImplementedError.
  - It launches on the transaction stream.
- **`DiskBatchJob.done()`** and **`DiskBatchJob.result(timeout=None)`.**
  `result()` raises RuntimeError on a read or launch failure, and
  TimeoutError while still pending.
- **`enqueue_lookups(bindings, token_counts, *, status_host,
  clear_tail=False) -> DiskBatchJob`**, in `engram._impl`, exported
  through `api` and the lazy `META.entry_points`. It is all-disk only. On
  the current stream it queues each table's transaction and `_stage_ids`
  ID copy, then one `launch_batch_read`, then the unchanged `lookup_op`
  per table. The ExitStack exit records `cache_done` on the same stream.
  There is no host wait.
- **`_stage_ids`** now also records `self._staged_count`; nothing else in
  existing Python changed.

### Native design

- **Read core.** `ple_read_core()` is the pinned `py_ple_reader_run`
  critical section with four mechanical substitutions (`.buf` removal and
  the failure out-parameter). A test checks this, and the refactored
  `ple_reader_run` is byte- and stats-identical to the pinned build.
- **Callback.** It runs table 0 on the driver callback thread and the
  other tables on per-step pthreads (joined), falling back to inline
  reads if thread creation fails. It then fences (`dsb sy` on aarch64,
  `mfence` on x86), does a seq_cst store of 1 or -1 into status, fences,
  and marks the job done under the job mutex with a condition broadcast.
  No Python, no CUDA, no frees.
- **Lifetime.**
  - The handle capsule owns the job. Python references are released only
    on a thread holding the GIL: at launch failure, once done
    (`result`), or in the capsule destructor.
  - A handle destroyed while its job is pending parks it on an orphan
    list with all references kept. Every `ple_batch` or `ple_batch_result`
    call sweeps finished orphans, unlinking them under the list mutex and
    freeing them after it, so deallocators can re-enter.
  - A skipped callback (context error) leaves the orphan parked: a
    bounded leak, never a use after free.
- **The transaction stays on the main thread.** The callback takes only
  each reader's native `api_mutex`, never the Python RLock. Nothing holds
  `api_mutex` while waiting on CUDA; `ple_reader_run` and `stats` hold it
  only around CPU work. `close()` waits on `cache_done`, which is recorded
  after the callback in stream order, before freeing mapped memory.
- **Status is never reset on the CPU.** Preparation and a pending launch
  leave it untouched (tested). Same-stream ordering puts each callback's
  store before that step's publication and after the previous step's.

### Tested

- **Builds.** Production and test variants both build with the loader's
  flags plus `-Werror`, and the production build references
  `cudaLaunchHostFunc`.
- **Callback behaviour.** The real callback ran on foreign pthreads. Both
  tables read correctly, and a read failure on one table stores -1 and
  reports "short PLE read" while the other table is still read.
- **Launch failure.** Everything is released immediately, and a relaunch
  is refused.
- **Pending and skipped jobs.** A handle dropped while pending keeps its
  buffers exported until the deferred callback runs, then is swept. A
  skipped callback stays parked with its buffers exported.
- **GIL independence.** The callback completes while the joining thread
  holds the GIL.
- **Validation.** Prepared-but-unlaunched and all validation paths.
- **Sanitizers.** The native suite is clean under AddressSanitizer and
  UBSan (`PLE_BATCH_SANITIZE=1`).
- **Python ordering.** The Python tests fix the exact order: enter and
  stage per table, one launch, lookups, then LIFO exits. A launch failure
  queues no lookup, and every refusal fires before any launch.

### Not tested; limits for vLLM integration

- **No real CUDA calls were made**, beyond the user's separate smoke:
  - host-function progress under the model's real cold-kernel conditions;
  - GPU visibility of full-size write-combined row writes;
  - behaviour next to NCCL and RoCEnante;
  - the aarch64 fence path (built and tested here on x86_64 only).
- **A hung disk read blocks the stream and the driver callback thread
  indefinitely.** io_uring waits have no timeout. The consumer timeout
  plus the fail-closed patch bound output correctness only.
- **Callbacks may serialize per context.** This design uses one callback
  per step. Thread creation per step costs are unmeasured.
- **Required sequencing for vLLM.**
  - Enter under `torch.cuda.stream(side)`.
  - Have the side stream wait on `ready` before `enqueue_lookups`.
  - Enqueue publication on the same stream after the call, conditioned
    on `status == 1`; status starts at whatever the caller initialized.
  - Keep the returned job, and call `result(timeout)` only where a host
    wait is acceptable.
- **Mapping is not checked.** `status_host` must be the host view of a
  mapped allocation for a GPU publication kernel to read it; the API
  cannot verify that.
- **Other limits.**
  - Free-threaded CPython is not supported.
  - The mixed disk and non-disk case is refused by `enqueue_lookups`.
  - Changing B12X bytes costs a full B12X recompile, and the loader
    extension rebuilds.

### Candidate B revision 2 and integration review (2026-09-25)

The native and B12X lock is now 334eedf4692c8be65832bfef7277aa67a4416dac8d46142f0ea5783684e4779e
(patch: 578 added lines). Suites:

| Suite | Tests | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| native | 16 | pass |
| native under AddressSanitizer and UBSan | 16 | clean |
| Python | 12 | pass |
| fail-closed | 14 + 11 | pass, unchanged |
| user's test_engram_progress_vllm (run read-only) | 4 | pass |

Fixes, from the user's review points:

1. **Overlap check.** `ple_batch_overlaps` now uses the same `uintptr_t`
   test as the pinned `ple_overlap`. The old version made relational
   comparisons of pointers into unrelated objects, which is undefined in
   C. A test covers boundaries: status ending exactly at rows start,
   the first aligned word after rows, and first, middle and last word
   overlaps.
2. **Timeouts.** NaN is refused, natively and in `DiskBatchJob.result`.
   +inf and anything above 1e9 s now wait indefinitely; the old code
   converted +inf to a timespec, which is undefined. Negative values wait
   natively, and the Python negative polls.
3. **Launch-failure wakeup.** The launch-failure path now broadcasts the
   condition variable. The state becomes LAUNCHED before the GIL is
   released around `cudaLaunchHostFunc`, so a concurrent
   `result(-1)` could wait forever. Negative control: without the
   broadcast the waiter was still blocked after 3 s; with it, it returned
   launch_failed at once. The test uses the new delayed-failure mode 5.
4. **Ownership made honest.** `result()` no longer releases the buffer
   exports; the job keeps them until its handle is dropped. The native
   comment and the `DiskBatchJob`, `launch_batch_read` and
   `enqueue_lookups` docstrings now state that the job keeps only what the
   host function touches, and only for it. Lookups and publication queued
   afterwards read mapped rows and status later, so the caller owns those
   lifetimes. A buffer export never governed the mapped allocations
   anyway: `MappedHostAllocation` frees on `close()` or `__del__`, which
   synchronizes the device.

User integration review of `prepare_engram_progress_vllm.py` and the
`engram-progress-*` files; no edits were made to them:

- **Ordering is correct.**
  - `_start_engram_job` fills expected and records ready on the main
    stream.
  - Then, on the side stream: wait on ready, `enqueue_lookups` (IDs,
    callback, lookups, cache_done), and `_publish_engram_epoch`.
  - All of this is queued before `prepare_disk_engram` returns and the
    forward is submitted.
  - Publication is gated on `status == 1`, written by this step's
    callback earlier in the same stream. Status is zeroed once and never
    reset.
  - The GPU gate receipt shows 8 of 8 cases passing, eager and captured.
- **Buffer reuse relies on GPU ordering, not the host wait.** Next-step
  main-stream writes (hashes, stage zeroing) follow forward k-1, whose
  wait observed publication after lookups k-1. On a timeout, the
  fail-closed check refuses outputs.
- **Finding 1 (liveness).** `_finish_engram_job` calls `job.result()` with
  no timeout. That is progress-safe normally: callback k-1 depends only on
  work before forward k-1. But a callback skipped after a context error,
  or a hung disk read, hangs prepare forever instead of erroring.
  Recommend a bounded `result(timeout=...)` raising TimeoutError. The
  wait is only for error reporting; reuse safety does not depend on it.
- **Finding 2 (unpinned dependency).** The integration lock pins the
  fail-closed lock but not this B12X lock, and `enqueue_lookups` exists
  only with it. Recommend pinning 334eedf4… in the integration lock.
- **Finding 3 (optional robustness).** `_publish_engram_epoch` loads
  status without `volatile=True`. GPU evidence covers io-error after
  success in both modes. A volatile load is cheap insurance against a
  cached stale line from mapped host memory.
- **Finding 4 (behaviour, not a bug).** A failed read leaves ready
  unpublished, so both Engram layers time out (about 10 s) before
  fail-closed refuses output. A fast-fail signal the wait kernel checks
  would shorten this. It is optional.

## Review: built Engram repair ee03505df7a3 and its GPU gates (2026-09-25, read-only)

No actionable defect was found in the built kit.

**Identity chain, verified:**

- **Native lock.** engram-repair.lock pins native lock 334eedf4..., which
  is the current claude-ple-batch.lock, and verifies every input.
- **Parent lock.** The integration lock pins the fail-closed lock
  4693deea... and the same native lock.
- **Untouched targets.** async_utils, model_runner and model_state are
  byte-identical to the reviewed fail-closed outputs.
- **Integration deltas.** The only differences are in model.py and
  engram.py: the publish kernel with a volatile status load, the mapped
  status allocation, the synchronous enqueue, `result(timeout=60)`, and
  no thread pool.
- **Installed files.** The installer checks base and preimage digests and
  confines changes to 11 targets. `verify_engram_repair` re-checks the
  installed outputs on GB10. The epoch gate's mounted kernel file hashes
  to 0d0fca1e..., which equals the installed engram.py target.
- **Environment.** The image env preloads NCCL 2.31.2 and sets
  `CUDA_MODULE_LOADING=LAZY`. Both are inherited from base e7 and apply
  equally in serving.

**Gate scope; not defects, but not covered:**

1. **The fail-closed path never ran on GPU.** The epoch and enqueue gates
   use a standalone flag tensor. None of the following ran: the flag in
   `engram_io_rows[0,0]`, the row all-reduce carrying it, the record ops,
   the snapshot copy, or the `get_output` raise. A qualification with no
   timeouts will not exercise it either.
2. **The enqueue gate uses `clear_tail=True`; production uses False.**
   Production does not depend on it: lookups write every row below
   prepared, and the consumer reads at most prepared rows. But the gate's
   zero and padding assertions describe the gate's mode, not production.
3. **The cold `torch.mm` trigger runs once.** It is only in the first
   run(False) epoch, and nothing proves the consumer was still spinning
   when the lazy load happened. The isolated reproducer covers the
   mechanism; the full LAZY boot is the real test.

**Qualification guards:**

- **Overlap is really on.** `VLLM_DS41_ENGRAM_OVERLAP` is unset, so it
  defaults to 1, and the disk backend defaults to io_uring (GDS is
  refused).
- **What a green run implies.** Under strict fail-closed, a green run
  implies no Engram timeout since boot, but only if item 1 works.
- **Independent timeout evidence.** Per-request `prefill_time` from the
  cache receipts: any short-prompt prefill of 5 s or more is red even if
  the outputs look right. The first cold 385 prefill was 7.0 s on e7 and
  should now be sub-second.
- **Close the run.** Finish with a final checked request.

### Proposed decisive cross-rank fail-closed test

It uses the candidate image, with no code change. Run it after
qualification; it ends that boot.

- **Why a throttle works.** The Engram reader opens its sources
  `O_DIRECT`, so a block-I/O throttle on one node applies to every row
  read. A throttled read longer than 5 s makes only that rank's consumer
  time out.
- **Prompt length.** Use more than 170 tokens (for example 2K). The Engram
  row reduce is then above RoCEnante's 2 MB cap and runs on NCCL, so the
  peers' 5 s wait cannot trip RoCEnante's `spin_limit` fail-stop
  (`DEFAULT_SPIN_LIMIT` is 20,000,000 polls).

Steps:

1. **Precheck on kirby (TP rank 3, not the output rank).** Confirm the
   rootless cgroup v2 io controller is delegated (`io` appears in the user
   manager's `cgroup.subtree_control`), and identify the device holding
   the HF cache.
2. **Control.** Send one request with a fresh random prompt of about 2K
   tokens and a new `cache_salt`. Expect success; record TTFT.
3. **Inject.** On kirby only, run
   `podman update --device-read-bps <dev>:65536 ds41-flash-karmic-main-tp4`,
   then send another fresh 2K prompt.

| Outcome | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| HTTP error, and dusty's log has "Model reported a fault in this step (fault epoch E, step epoch E)" | PASS: a rank-3 timeout was carried to rank 0 through the real reduce, record, snapshot and `get_output` |
| Success with TTFT of 5 s or more | FAIL: fail-closed is broken |
| Success with TTFT under 5 s | Inconclusive: the injection did not take effect |
| Error from a RoCEnante, NCCL or other failure | Confounded |

4. **Teardown.** Stop gracefully (`podman stop -t 60`); the throttle ends
   with the container.

**Fallback if io delegation is absent:** a diagnostic derivative image,
not the candidate, with an env-gated one-shot skip of
`_publish_engram_epoch` on one named TP rank, triggered by a file in
/cache. Use the same outcome table.

### Correction to the ee03505df7a3 review (NCCL)

My note that the image's NCCL 2.31.2 preload "applies equally in serving"
was wrong. `launch_contract.py` sets `LD_PRELOAD` and `VLLM_NCCL_SO_PATH` to
`/opt/nccl-2.30.7/lib/libnccl.so.2.30.7` with library prefix
`/opt/nccl-2.30.7/lib`, and `gate_ds41.py` enforces it. Serving uses stock
2.30.7. Only the single-GPU build gates ran with the image default 2.31.2,
and they make no collective calls.

## Cross-rank fail-closed diagnostic: one-shot publication skip (2026-09-25; proposal, not built)

Kirby exposes only cpu, memory and pids cgroup controllers, so the
io-throttle test is not possible without a host policy change.

**No cheaper full-path alternative exists.** The candidate's launch
contract enforces its environment and kit digest, so nothing can be
injected into a running candidate without code or a host policy change. A
4-node harness running one Engram layer with the real TP all-reduce,
snapshot and `get_output` would skip weight loading, but it would not run
the real scheduler, executor and engine failure path, and it still
occupies all four nodes. A diagnostic boot is the decisive test.

**Artifacts** (lock sha256 957f7e24d2a8e715...):

| File | Role |
| --- | --- |
| claude_engram_fault_inject.py | the diagnostic helper |
| claude_prepare_engram_fault_inject.py | the preparer |
| claude-engram-fault-inject-model.py and claude-engram-fault-inject.patch | patched model and diff |
| claude-engram-fault-inject.lock.json and .ignore | lock and build context |
| Dockerfile.claude-engram-fault-inject and claude_install_engram_fault_inject.py | recipe over ee03505df7a3 |
| claude_classify_fault_inject.py | outcome classifier |
| claude_test_engram_fault_inject.py | 18 stdlib tests, passing |

The base is engram-progress-model.py `e8c08fa6...`, which equals the
repair lock's installed model.py. The candidate's production files and the
repair lock inputs are verified unchanged.

**Change.** In `_start_engram_job`, `should_skip(epoch, rows)` is decided
on the host after `stream = self._engram_stream()` and before any CUDA work
of the job. Only the `_publish_engram_epoch` launch is guarded; reads and
lookups are queued as usual. One helper module is added. B12X bytes are
unchanged, so the candidate's B12X cache and native loader build are
reused. vLLM and Inductor caches go to a separate directory.

**Safety.** It fires at most once per process and once per token, only
if all of these hold:

- **Fresh trigger.** The trigger's mtime is after the helper's import, so
  a stale file from an earlier boot cannot fire during warmup.
- **Targeting.** The trigger names `rank 3` and `node kirby`, matched
  against the worker's TP rank and `DS41_NODE`.
- **Prefill only.** The job has at least `min_tokens` rows, and the trigger
  must set `min_tokens` to at least 171. Decode never fires, and the
  delayed reduce is above RoCEnante's 2 MiB cap, so it runs on NCCL.
- **Durable receipt first.** A receipt
  `/cache/ds41-engram-fault-inject-<token>.consumed` is published
  atomically: fsynced temporary file, `os.link` to the final name, then a
  directory fsync. It records epoch, rows, rank, node, pid and times.
- **Fail safe.** Any error, an invalid trigger, or a receipt that could not
  be written means no skip.

**Procedure,** after candidate qualification, under a separate diagnostic
boot with the same profile:

1. Once ready, send a fresh 2K-token control request. Expect success.
2. On kirby only, write the trigger atomically with a new token, rank 3,
   node kirby and min_tokens 1024, into
   `~/.cache/vllm-jj-ds41-tp4/ds41-engram-fault-inject.json`. Wait 2 s.
3. Send one fresh 2K-token prompt with no concurrent traffic. Save
   `{status, elapsed_s, body}`.
4. Collect kirby's receipt, kirby's "fired" log line, and dusty's container
   log since the request started.
5. Run `claude_classify_fault_inject.py`. PASS requires all three:
   - the receipt epoch E;
   - no generated text;
   - dusty logging "Model reported a fault in this step (fault epoch E,
     step epoch E)" before any confounder.

   The confounders are:
   - a RoCE collective timeout or proxy failure;
   - NCCL error or watchdog messages;
   - the Engram read timeout or failure messages;
   - CUDA errors;
   - a fault message naming a different epoch.

   The other verdicts are FAIL, CONFOUNDED, NOT-FIRED, MISTARGETED (the job
   is clearly larger than the prompt) and INCONCLUSIVE.
6. Stop gracefully, move the trigger aside (the durable receipt already
   blocks refiring), and return to the unmodified candidate.

### Fault-inject kit revision (2026-09-25, after user review)

The lock is now 54965c1221a08ca2e5d562f1b0b65e4fe55e19de5a2b0d55e5a1f8c2511aa5f0.
Tests: 23 stdlib, passing. The patched model.py (97aa32a8...) is unchanged;
only the helper and classifier changed.

- **Earlier faults.** The first model fault in rank 0's log must be "this
  step" with fault and step epoch equal to the receipt epoch. Any earlier
  fault, whether a different epoch or "an earlier step", is CONFOUNDED.
  Later echoes are allowed.
- **Proven rejection.** `response_outcome()` returns served, rejected or
  unproven.
  - Only HTTP 4xx or 5xx, or a recorded transport error with no status,
    counts as rejected.
  - A 2xx response counts as served when any choice carries content,
    reasoning_content, reasoning, tool_calls, function_call or text,
    checked in the choice, message and delta. Served means FAIL.
  - A 2xx with no output, a non-JSON 2xx, and any other status are
    unproven, which means INCONCLUSIVE.
  - Requests must be non-streaming.
- **Receipt writes.** `_write_all` loops over short writes, and a write
  that makes no progress raises. That means no skip and no receipt, and the
  temporary file is removed.
- **Trigger stat.** `os.stat` of the trigger now runs inside the fail-safe
  try. A missing file stays silent; PermissionError or any other OSError
  means no skip.
- **Negative tests added** for: reasoning-only and tool-call-only 2xx;
  2xx with empty, missing, or non-JSON bodies plus a fault log; 503;
  transport errors with and without text; 302; an earlier different-epoch
  fault; an earlier "an earlier step" fault; later echoes; 5-byte short
  writes; zero-progress writes; stat PermissionError and EIO.

## Review: repaired-candidate qualification and the residual 255 divergence (2026-09-25, read-only)

### Evidence

- **Short receipt `engram-repair-20260925T182128Z`.** 385, 128 and 16384,
  six cold salted trials each, are all correct and bitwise identical. The
  first request after boot was 385-0 (14.6 s prefill), and it matched its
  repeats. No model-fault, RoCE, NCCL or JIT-monitor message appeared on
  any rank.
- **Residual 255.** In the qualification sweep, 255 repeat 5 differs. In
  the 100-repeat stress run, repeats 23 and 28 differ; 98 are identical.
  - Divergence starts at the prefill output (response token 0): the chosen
    token is the same, but shared top-20 logprobs move 1.1 to 1.25 nats.
    Later positions move up to 1.75.
  - Repeat 23 also accepted one draft token instead of two, a consequence
    of the different logits.
- **Timing correlation.** Stress prefill is about 0.25 s at the median. A
  contiguous window, repeats 21 to 31, ran about 0.55 s, and both
  divergent repeats fall inside it. During that window all four nodes
  dropped from about 39 W to about 29 W together. There was no direct
  reclaim, no NVRM error in any kernel log, and MemAvailable was steady.
  The slowdown is synchronized and its cause is not visible in telemetry.
- **Length uniqueness.** 255 is the only swept length congruent to -1
  modulo 16, 64, 128 or 256. The sweep cannot separate "exactly 255"
  from "last token just before a 16, 128 or 256 boundary". Lengths 255 and
  256 are the only ones whose first 8-token verify straddles 256.

### Findings

1. **Residual 255 nondeterminism blocks qualification.** It is prefill,
   rare (about 2 to 3 percent), length-specific in the tested set, and
   clusters in a slow window, so it is timing-correlated.
2. **Fail-closed has not been exercised on GPU.** No request failed after
   repeats 5, 23 and 28, which is consistent with those not being Engram
   timeouts, but only if fail-closed works. Run the cross-rank injection
   before claiming it.
3. **Qualification driver: container identity covers only the middle
   stage.** `qualify_dense_release` checks StartedAt, Image and
   RestartCount before and after its own stages. The transition,
   conversations and final-check stages are outside that span, so the
   final "sticky-fault checked output" is not proven to share a process
   with the earlier stages. Wrap the whole driver in before and after
   identity checks.
4. **Repeat depth is too shallow for this failure rate.** With 6 repeats a
   2 to 3 percent residual escapes a given length about 83 to 89 percent
   of the time. It was caught here because 28 lengths ran. Add a targeted
   stress stage.
5. **No independent timing guard.** Request 385-1 had TTFT 7.08 s against
   1.70 s of prefill and 0 s queue: about 5.4 s before scheduling, just
   after the first request. Strict fail-closed says it was not an Engram
   timeout, but it is unexplained. Record TTFT, prefill and queue per
   request and flag gaps of 5 s or more.
6. **Memory pressure (pre-existing, not from the repair).** During the
   first requests of the ring-fix, timing and repair boots, MemAvailable
   fell to 0.36 to 2 GB. Direct reclaim ran on every node (hundreds of
   thousands to 1.6 million pages scanned, 500 to 3,000 stalls), with
   occasional in-request `NV_ERR_NO_MEMORY`. Startup NVRM bursts reached
   363 on dusty. The NVMe re-reads during the first request fit
   page-cache refault. It is an envelope risk and a timing perturbation.

The `check_transition` logic and its tests are sound. The unchanged
correctness chain (28-length repeatability, semantic, original 524K, long
repeatability, context) is intact inside `qualify_dense_release`.

### Isolating residual 255, cheapest first

The current boot is acceptable where noted.

- **A. Length map, interleaved in random order**, so a time window cannot
  masquerade as a length effect. About 50 repeats each at:
  - 247 to 258;
  - 127, 128, 383, 384;
  - 511, 512, 767, 1023;
  - 143 and 271, as the 15-mod-16 control.

  Pattern read-out: only 255 means an exact-shape tile or tail path;
  every value that is -1 mod 256 means block or partial-state; -1 mod 128
  means C128 partial group; 15 mod 16 means a ring or 16-slot structure.
  No restart needed.
- **B. Prior-content control at 255.** Use the transition tooling with 255
  targets after different primes: 385, 16K, and 255 with other content.
  Content dependence points to reading unwritten or stale slots whose
  contents vary with async look-ahead or decode history. The ring bug had
  this signature, and the existing transition gate covers only 385.
- **C. Timing perturbation.** Repeat the 255 stress with steady background
  traffic or host CPU load. A rising rate points to a cross-stream or
  host-timing race; a flat rate points to content or kernel-internal
  causes.
- **D. Localization.** Only after A to C narrow it. A diagnostic image
  records per-layer, per-rank activation checksums on device during
  prefill, copied once at the end of the request. Compare divergent and
  modal vectors to find the first diverging layer and rank. For
  stale-state suspicion, use the existing stale-state probe at 255. For
  exact-shape suspicion, run the single-GPU dense and MoE replay matrix at
  M=255 and its siblings under load.

Discriminating arms, which need a restart and the user's decision: async
scheduling off, and speculative decoding off. They separate the
look-ahead and draft-state writers from the prefill kernels.

**Future enhancements, not blockers:** a fast-fail publication code for
native read failures, so the consumer does not wait 5 s, and removing the
per-step host `result()` wait now that ordering does not rely on it.

## Residual prefill divergence: activation-digest localization (2026-09-25; proposal, not built)

**Updated evidence (user):**

- Length 254 diverged 1 of 100 and 256 diverged 1 of 100.
- At 255 with `max_tokens=1`, 3 of 100 diverged, so later generated tokens
  are not needed.
- Every anomaly fell in a slower timing window.
- The interleaved map, after 352 requests over 16 cycles, has non-modal
  results at 258 and 1023 and no unexplained TTFT of 5 s or more.

Conclusion so far: this is a low-rate, timing-correlated prefill race
spread across lengths, not a 255-specific or boundary-specific defect.

**Artifacts** (lock sha256 194ce1bd...):

| File | Role |
| --- | --- |
| claude_act_trace.py | the diagnostic helper |
| claude_act_analyze.py | stdlib offline analyzer |
| claude_prepare_act_trace.py | the preparer |
| claude-act-trace-model.py and claude-act-trace.patch | patched model and diff |
| claude-act-trace.lock.json and .ignore | lock and build context |
| Dockerfile.claude-act-trace and claude_install_act_trace.py | recipe over ee03505df7a3 |
| claude_test_act_trace.py | 21 tests, passing under .venv-snapshot-cpu |

The base is engram-progress-model.py `e8c08fa6...`. The patch adds one
import and one `_act_trace.install(self)` as the last statement of
`DeepseekV4Model.__init__`. B12X is unchanged.

**Design:**

- **Step hooks.** Two hooks on the target DeepseekV4Model run every eager
  forward. Module hooks are registered only while
  `/cache/ds41-act-trace.json` exists (re-read on mtime change), and
  removed when it disappears.
- **Skipped steps.** Steps under CUDA graph capture or compile are
  skipped, and graph replays never run Python.
- **Trace window.** Only forwards whose row count is within
  `[min_tokens, max_tokens]` (at most 2048) are traced, so MTP verify and
  look-ahead steps are excluded. The draft model is not traced.
- **Default hooks.**
  - Outputs of `layers.*` (all 5 tuple members), `layers.*.attn`,
    `layers.*.ffn` and `layers.*.engram`.
  - Inputs of `layers.*.attn.wo_b`, which are rank-local before the TP
    reduction if that module is invoked.
  - A step key made from the digests of `input_ids` and `positions`.
- **Pattern matching.** Each dotted segment matches exactly one name
  segment. A test caught that plain fnmatch `*` matched dots and would have
  hooked every nested submodule.
